100. **Three measurement decisions that changed a Phase E answer, recorded because each was a wrong answer first.** (a) *Levels are quoted against the loudest transverse partial, not the fundamental.* A0's fundamental is 43 dB below its own third partial, and quoted against it the residuals read as louder than the note. (b) *The census sees only the band the note occupies* (`0.75 f_1` to 12 kHz). Below the fundamental a recording holds room rumble and the microphone's offset — peaks at 4 to 20 Hz that are louder than a treble note's partials, and that were duly reported as the loudest unexplained content of C7 until the band was applied. (c) *The mechanism recordings are compared at the level the SFZ plays them*, not as stored: the key-off group carries `volume=-37` and the pedal groups -19 and -20 (`SalamanderGrandPiano-V3+20200602.sfz` lines 593, 686, 691). Compared raw, a damper landing measures as loud as the note it damps.

101. **Phase E's code is a module and an example, not a subcommand.** `tuner/src/residual.rs` holds the measurements as pure functions with ten unit tests (the glide of a synthetic sliding partial, a phantom that must not be mistaken for the transverse partial beside it, a window skirt that must not be reported as unexplained content, a band split against a known floor, a stereo balance, a transient's decay and colour, an excitation scatter that is zero on a spectrum the comb explains); `tuner/examples/residuals.rs` drives them over the library and over the engine's renders and prints the tables the report is written from. An example rather than a `piano-tuner` subcommand for one reason: the control renders need the engine, and the engine is a *dev*-dependency of the tuner (item at `tuner/Cargo.toml`) so that the library stays usable without an audio device on the link line. The trajectory pass reads the same cache the survey wrote, so no number in the report re-derives anything Phase D measured.

102. **`Preset::validate` now covers the global voicing, and both crates' copies of it are asserted against each other.** Item 52 states the contract — a preset is validated when it is loaded, not when it is played — and the check did not meet it: it tested the *lengths* of `voicing.horizontal_offset_hz` and of the `unison_layout` rows and never the values inside them, and it did not look at `unison_coupling` or `resonance_coupling` at all. `ResonanceBus::new` clamps its coupling, but `NaN.clamp(0.0, MAX_COUPLING)` is `NaN`, and TOML accepts `nan` as a float, so a one-word edit to a preset file was enough: a `nan` in the first `horizontal_offset_hz` loaded cleanly and the first note-on filled the render with NaN — on the live path, the device. The gaps are now closed with the bounds the DSP actually needs rather than with a blanket finiteness test: each horizontal offset within ±the lowest fundamental in the tuning (a larger negative shift puts a first partial at a negative frequency), each `unison_layout` detune within ±1 of the group's width and each share within 0..=the row's string count (the shares split one blow and a hammer cannot pull), both couplings within `0..=MAX_UNISON_COUPLING` / `MAX_COUPLING` — they are loop gains, and clamping silently is the wrong answer for a number a human typed — and the `damper_weight` anchors strictly ascending in hertz, which is the precondition `types::interp_anchors` documents and `damper_weight_at` relies on (a repeated frequency divides by a zero span). The same checks are mirrored into `tuner/src/preset.rs`, and `preset::tests::both_crates_refuse_the_same_broken_voicing` builds each broken preset once and asserts *both* crates refuse it — the tuner's own `validate` on the struct and the engine's on the TOML the tuner writes — so item 76's duplication is now pinned on rejection as well as on round trip. `presets/salamander-c5.toml` passes unchanged.

103. **A preset may leave out a field whose neutral value is the old model, and the engine does not write it back.** Phase E's string-side findings each add a parameter with a setting that means "as before": `notes.inharmonicity_b4` at zero is the two-parameter partial law, `notes.contact_width` at zero is the point-force strike comb, `voicing.unison_sigma_scale` at one is the shared damping law, `voicing.polarization_pan_spread` at zero is one pan position per key. All four are `#[serde(default)]` on the way in and `skip_serializing_if` on the way out while they hold that value. Three things follow, and all three were the reason. (a) `presets/default.toml` and `presets/salamander-c5.toml` are still character for character what the engine writes — `preset::tests::the_checked_in_default_is_character_for_character_what_the_engine_writes` now asserts that directly rather than asserting equality of the parsed structs, which is the stronger claim and the one item 76's `tuner/src/preset.rs` round trip depends on: the tuner's copy of the schema is `deny_unknown_fields`, so a field the engine started emitting would break the file that is the interface between the two crates. (b) The tuner needs no change at all until it has something to write into these fields, and a preset it writes today loads into the new engine as the instrument it describes. (c) `validate` treats them exactly as it treats the mandatory tables — length, range, and for `inharmonicity_b4` the series it produces — so "optional in the file" nowhere becomes "unchecked in the engine". The neutral defaults were verified to be neutral by rendering, not by reasoning: `piano-emulator render demo` and `render` (the compass sweep) built from the commit before this work and from the finished engine are byte-identical WAVs (md5 `7d369e28…` and `0e90ebe1…`), which is what "reduces exactly to the old law" has to mean for four changes that all touch the string's construction. The one thing the convention costs is discoverability — `piano-emulator preset my.toml` no longer dumps every field the engine understands — and the module doc lists the four in exchange.

104. **The partial law gains a signed fourth-order coefficient, and refusing the presets it cannot play is most of the work.** `f_k = k f0 sqrt(1 + B k^2 + B4 k^4)`, per `TUNING_REPORT.md` §1: one `B` misplaces a wound bass string's partials by up to 78 cents, and the sign of the error flips between the wound bass (fitted `B` falls 25–37 % along the series) and the short wound tenor strings (it rises 24–45 %), so a one-sided correction is refuted before it is built. `StringParams::partial_radicand` is factored out of `partial_freq` and evaluates `1.0 + b * k * k + b4 * k2 * k2` with the first term associated exactly as the two-parameter law wrote it, so a preset with `B4 = 0` adds a literal `+ 0.0` and lays its partials out bit for bit as before (`string::tests::a_zero_fourth_order_coefficient_is_the_two_parameter_law_to_the_bit`, all 88 keys, `assert_eq` on the float). The real cost is that `k^4` is a truncated expansion and a negative coefficient always turns the top of the series over: `Preset::validate` therefore walks every key's *used* partial range — `1..=partial_count()`, the modes the bank will actually hold — and refuses a radicand that is not positive or a frequency that is not above its predecessor, naming the note. That bites harder than expected and is worth recording as a limit on what stage 2 can fit: A0 is built with the full 80 partials, so the largest legal negative `B4` there takes only ~7.5 % off `B` by the twentieth partial, against the ~25 % the recordings want. One `k^4` term buys part of §1's shape at A0, not all of it; buying the rest needs either a shorter partial cap in the bass or a law with a different tail, and the engine now says which one it is refusing rather than rendering a NaN.

105. **The strings of a unison get individual decay rates, as a row of multipliers averaging to one.** `voicing.unison_sigma_scale` mirrors `unison_layout` — one row per group size, row *n* with *n* entries — and `PianoString::new` multiplies each string's sigma by its entry, both polarizations together. The finding it serves is `TUNING_REPORT.md` §6: the measured fundamental of some tenor notes drifts as it decays (F#3 by 32 cents over its first 20 dB) where the engine's moves 0.2, and what moves a composite partial's frequency is a unison whose strings are mistuned *and* decay at different rates, so that the survivor's pitch takes over — the engine's group could not do it because its strings shared one damping law. The row is constrained to average 1 (to 1e-3) and each entry to 0.5..=2.0, which is the decision worth recording: without the mean constraint the field is a second decay control sitting on top of `notes.sigma0`, and fitting it would quietly retune the whole compass's T60 away from the anchors item 30 calibrated. With it, the field redistributes a note's damping and cannot change how long the note rings — and at all ones it multiplies by exactly 1.0, which is why `string::tests::fundamental_t60_matches_the_spec_anchors` and the rendered T60 of `acceptance::the_fundamental_decays_over_a_pianistic_time` are untouched. `unison_sigma_scale_sets_each_string_of_a_group_going_at_its_own_rate` measures the rendered per-string decay for `[1, 1, 1]` and for `[0.7, 1, 1.3]` with the bridge coupling off, and finds each string within 5 % of its own designed rate.

106. **A voice may place its two polarizations at different points in the stereo image, and pays for the split render only when it does.** `voicing.polarization_pan_spread` puts the horizontal polarization at `pan + spread` and the vertical one at `pan - spread`, with the sign alternating by key parity so that 88 voices leaving their aftersound on the same side of themselves do not add up to a lateral bias. `TUNING_REPORT.md` §5 is the reason: the per-partial left-minus-right balance of a recorded note drifts 1.2–6.2 dB between 0.3 s and 2 s while the engine's drifts 0.02–0.14, and the engine's is zero for a structural reason — it pans one mono voice per key, so the balance it produces cannot change as the note decays, whatever the pan. Because the two polarizations decay at very different rates, giving them different positions makes the image travel from the fast one's place to the slow one's for free. Two implementation decisions. First, `PianoString::process_split` renders the two polarizations into separate buffers but advances the group exactly as `process` does — same excitation, same one-block-late bridge coupling, same state, the two planes summed again before they drive it — so the spread is a way of *reading* the group, not a second group (`splitting_the_polarizations_renders_the_same_string`). Second, `process` stays and is what a voice with no spread calls: the summed path adds the polarizations per string in string order, the split path adds all the verticals and then all the horizontals, and those are not the same float, so keeping both is what lets the shipped instrument's render stay bit-identical rather than merely equal to a rounding. The mono sum the voice hands back is unchanged either way, so the sympathetic bus and the halo cannot be moved by the stereo image (`the_pan_spread_leaves_the_voices_mono_sum_alone`). `Voice::process` now takes the `Soundboard` and places itself on it — only the voice knows whether it is one signal or two — and `Engine::process_block` just feeds the bus. Measured end to end on the default preset at the report's own metric (fundamental balance at 0.3 s against 2 s, held C4): 0.17 dB of drift at spread 0, 7.9 dB at spread 0.4, and the neighbouring key drifts the other way. The ceiling is 0.4 because `soundboard::MAX_PAN` is 0.6 and the sum is the edge of the stage.

107. **The strike comb is multiplied by a contact taper, evaluated once at construction.** `PHYSICS.md` §7: the mode input gain `sin(k pi x_strike)` is a point force, while a real hammer touches 1–2 % of the speaking length, so the comb should be convolved with the contact profile — `cos^2(k pi w / 2)` for a raised-cosine patch. `notes.contact_width` carries `w` per note and `string::contact_taper` is clamped to zero past the patch's first null, where the analytic form turns back up: a contact patch that spans a whole half-period of a partial cannot drive it, and cannot start driving it again by getting wider. Zero width returns exactly 1.0 and the gain is multiplied by it, so the default instrument's comb is untouched to the bit. The ceiling is 5 % of the speaking length, well past any measured felt and the point where the twentieth partial is nulled outright. This is the cheapest of the four changes and the one with the least evidence behind its *value* — §7's own evidence rule asks Phase E for a monotone high-frequency droop in the comb residual, and `TUNING_REPORT.md` §3 found the residual to be per-note and per-partial rather than a smooth droop — so it ships as a parameter at its neutral value rather than as a voiced default, for the estimator (which reads the same spectrum for the felt stiffness) to fit or to leave alone.

108. **The instrument makes its own noises now, and `[noise]` is the one preset section whose default is a measurement rather than a neutral value.** `engine/src/noise.rs`: white noise through two band-pass sections and a fixed ~2 kHz low pass, under an exponential envelope, one burst per event. Four events — `key_off`, `damper_lift`, `pedal_down`, `pedal_up` — each with a `centroid_hz`, a `decay_s` (time to −40 dB, which is how `TUNING_REPORT.md` §5 measured it), a `velocity_db` tracking slope and a `level_db` table anchored across the compass in dB *relative to a velocity-90 strike of the same key*. The defaults are §5's second table entry for entry: A0 −37.3, A3 −30.2, C4 −35.4, C5 −25.4, C7 −33.5, centroid 190 Hz, 0.24 s; pedal-down −35.8 dB, 5.76 s, 77 Hz; pedal-up −42.4 dB, 0.32 s, 187 Hz. The 2 kHz ceiling is not tabulated because it is a property of the structure rather than of the event: Askenfelt's dummy-mass measurement (`PHYSICS.md` §5) puts the whole structure-borne spectrum below it. Unlike items 103's four fields, the *neutral* setting of this section is not what it defaults to — the report's finding was that the engine made **no** sound at a release, and that was the error — so the default is the measured action and a preset that wants the old silence has to say so, by writing the section with its levels far down (`preset::tests::an_action_that_is_not_the_measured_one_is_written_in_full`). It is still skipped on the way out while it equals the measured table, for exactly item 103's reason: the file is the interface to `tuner/src/preset.rs`, which is `deny_unknown_fields`, and `presets/default.toml` is byte for byte what the engine writes.

109. **Mechanism noise is deterministic, seeded per event from key and frame, with no generator state shared between voices.** Each `Burst` carries its own 32-bit xorshift word and is reseeded at every trigger from `noise::seed_of(key, frame)`, where `frame` is the engine's own block counter. Three things this buys, and each was a requirement rather than a nicety. (a) A render is reproducible: the same event list produces the same samples, live or offline, so `render_to_wav` remains a thing you can diff and the tuner's estimators keep working on engine renders. (b) Two keys released together get uncorrelated bursts rather than one burst at two levels, which would sum as a single louder thump rather than as a chord being let go — the key and the frame are each mixed before they meet, for that reason. (c) There is no global RNG on the audio path to lock, to allocate, or to make two voices' outputs depend on the order they were rendered in. `Burst::add` is a fixed loop over the block with five multiply-adds per sample and no branches; a voice with no burst running does not enter it, which is what keeps `engine::tests::an_idle_engine_renders_exact_silence` and `voice::tests::an_idle_voice_writes_no_samples_at_all` bit-exact rather than merely quiet.

110. **The noise reaches the soundboard directly, at the key's pan, and never the resonance bus.** A key-off thump travels to the ear through the keybed and the board, not along the string, so it is summed into the soundboard's input rather than into the voice's own block: `Voice::process` adds it with a second `add_voice` call at the key's pan and leaves `out` — the signal the sympathetic bus reads and the next block's coupling drives on — untouched. Panned per key rather than centred because Fontana et al. (2024) showed listeners identify *which key* was played from the mechanical noise alone; the pedal tray is the exception and is centred, being one object at the middle of the instrument. This also means a voice whose strings are silent still has something to render — a key released long after its note died still thumps — so the noise is rendered on the idle early-return path as well, before the voice reports that it has nothing for the bus.

111. **`NoteOn` splits at an escapement threshold and `NoteOff` carries a release velocity.** `Event::NoteOn` with `vel <= ESCAPEMENT_VELOCITY` (3) lifts the damper and throws no hammer, and `Event::KeyDown` says the same thing without going through a velocity, for callers that have the gesture rather than a number; the REPL spells them `hold <note>` and MIDI needs no new message, since a file can simply carry a very quiet note-on. `Event::NoteOff` gains `vel`, which sets the damper's ramp geometrically about the 10 ms the engine has always used — 50 ms at release velocity 1, 2 ms at 127, and *exactly* 10 ms at 64, so a source with no release velocity plays the instrument that was there before. MIDI reads a note-off velocity of 0 as 64 rather than as the slowest possible release, because that is what a keyboard that cannot measure one sends, and a note-off written as a note-on with velocity 0 has no release velocity to carry at all. `pedal.rs` needed no change: sostenuto captures whatever `Engine::held` says is down, and a silent press sets it (`engine::tests::sostenuto_captures_a_key_that_was_pressed_without_striking`, both spellings). The acceptance test for the whole thing is `acceptance::a_silently_held_key_answers_a_struck_note_with_the_pedal_up` — C3 prepared silently picks up a hundred times the energy from a struck G4 that a C3 at rest does, and its own string is measured at exactly zero, so the preparation really was silent.

112. **The damper lift is fired on a silent press and deliberately not under a strike.** `damper_lift` is the felt leaving the string, which physically happens at every key-down; it is modelled only where it is audible. Under a hammer blow it is 30 dB down and inaudible — no sample library records one, for that reason — and putting it there is not free: fired on every note-on, it moved the tuner's self-calibration gate (`tuner/tests/calibration.rs`, C6's sixth partial) past its 1-cent threshold, because a broadband burst inside the first 80 ms is exactly what every estimator that reads an engine render has to work around. Where it *is* the whole sound is the silent press, so that is where it fires. `key_off` conversely fires on every key including the ones above the damper break, because `rel76` is C7 — five semitones past G6, and one of the loudest of the measured releases: what stops at the break is the damper, not the key.

113. **The pedal tray sounds on the crossing, not on the position, and is scaled by how many dampers actually move.** The sustain pedal is continuous and the dampers follow it continuously, but the rumble belongs to the gesture: `Engine::tray_noise` fires only when the value crosses 0.5, up or down, so a Disklavier recording's stream of CC 64 values above the threshold makes one sound rather than a hundred (`engine::tests::the_pedal_tray_sounds_once_per_crossing` asserts a worked pedal renders bit for bit what a held one does). Its drive is the fraction of the seventy damped keys that are neither held by a key nor caught by sostenuto — the dampers the pedal has left to move — so a pedal pressed under a fully held keyboard is nearly silent and one pressed over an empty one moves everything, which is `PHYSICS.md` §5's "one shared event scaled by how many dampers actually moved". Measured end to end against §5: pedal-down lands at −34.8 dB relative to a velocity-90 strike against the recording's −35.8, pedal-up at −42.3 against −42.4.

114. **An event's level is a peak, so its normalisation is measured over a whole enveloped burst — and averaged over draws, because the peak of a noise band is itself a random number.** `TUNING_REPORT.md` §5 reports peak levels, and the peak of a filtered decaying noise burst is not available in closed form. `EventShape::new` therefore runs the finished chain — envelope included — for a second of samples and normalises against the result. Including the envelope is the part that matters: a band-pass at 120 Hz needs a couple of cycles to ring up, by which time a 0.24 s envelope has already lost 2 dB, so normalising against the filter's steady state alone leaves every event quiet by an amount that depends on its own centroid (measured: 2.5 dB for the key-off, 5 dB for the pedal-down rumble). The peak is averaged over eight seeds because a 100 Hz band under a 0.2 s envelope only gets a couple of dozen independent excursions and their maximum scatters by 2–3 dB; the *design* level is then exact while each individual event still lands where its own seed puts it, which is a feature — no two releases of a real key are identical, and it is why `acceptance::a_note_off_thumps_at_the_level_the_recordings_measured` allows each key 6 dB and the mean across the compass 2.5. The four shapes are built once per engine and copied into the voices (`NoiseShapes`), since normalising is the one expensive thing in `Engine::new`; 88 voices building their own would have cost 34 million filter steps per engine, and every offline render builds one.

115. **A spectral centroid is a first moment in linear frequency and a resonator is symmetric in log frequency, so the band-pass is tuned below the centroid the preset asks for.** `CENTROID_WARP = 0.92`: the skirt above a resonance covers more hertz than the one below it, so a band-pass centred at `f` returns a centroid about 9 % above `f`. The constant is measured rather than derived, and `noise::tests::the_burst_lands_near_the_centroid_the_preset_asks_for` checks the finished chain against §5's own 77–261 Hz column rather than against the constant — so if the filter topology changes, the test fails on the claim that matters instead of passing on an internal number. The same test pins the 2 kHz band limit at 60 dB of rejection an octave and a half above a 500 Hz burst.

116. **A partly-engaged damper soft-limits the string's bridge force, geometrically in the damper's position, against the level the note had when the felt arrived.** `PHYSICS.md` §6, from Lehtonen, Askenfelt & Välimäki's measurement of a part-pedalled note: free vibration, damper-string interaction, free vibration again, with the middle interval decaying fast *and* changing the timbre because the felt limits the string's deflection nonlinearly at its position. `Voice` keeps a fast peak follower of its own output while the damper is clear of the string and freezes it when the damper starts to arrive; the limiter's threshold is that level times `FELT_CLEARANCE.powf(damper_current)`, i.e. the full swing at a lifted damper falling geometrically to 1 % at a seated one, so a half pedal sits at a tenth of the swing. The third interval falls out rather than being scheduled: the note decays under the (linear) extra damping until it stops meeting the felt. Cost is one block peak and, only for a voice actually in contact, one `powf` and a `tanh` per sample — every other voice on the instrument executes the peak and returns. The acceptance test is an *exact* control rather than an approximate one: half-pedalling multiplies the damper's extra decay rate by the pedal position, so an instrument with `damper_sigma` halved and its dampers fully down has, partial by partial, precisely the damping of the first at half pedal, and no felt in contact anywhere. Same note, same linear damping, mechanism silenced and the sympathetic bus uncoupled so that the pedal move cannot answer instead — the half-pedalled note comes back 5.6 dB brighter in the 1.5–6 kHz band relative to the fundamental region, which is the opposite direction from anything a linear damper can do (the felt's frequency response grips low partials hardest).

117. **The felt's correction is not injected back into the string's excitation, and the reason is dimensional rather than aesthetic.** `PHYSICS.md` §6 describes the nonlinear contact as "one soft clipper on the string's summed output, fed back as a correction". The limiter is on the string's force on the bridge, which is what the board radiates *and* what the sympathetic bus carries to the rest of the instrument, so the correction does reach everything downstream of the string. Feeding it into the string's own modal excitation was built, measured and removed: an excitation and an output differ by the mode's input gain (`output_scale * sin(k pi x) / 48000`, about 4e-6) times the mode's accumulation time, so injecting an output-scale correction as an excitation is roughly 10^5 too small — at a feedback coefficient of 1.0 the rendered result was bit-identical to no feedback at all, and reaching audibility would have meant a per-block-delayed nonlinear loop with a gain in the thousands through 88 modal banks. Converting a force at the damper's position into a modal drive needs the string's admittance rather than a constant; that is a real piece of physics and it is not this one. Recorded because the alternative is a constant in the code that looks like it does something.

118. **The tuner mirrors the four refinement fields and `[noise]`, and what keeps the two copies honest is now thirty-seven breakages and a second round trip.** `tuner/src/preset.rs` gains `notes.inharmonicity_b4`, `notes.contact_width`, `voicing.polarization_pan_spread`, `voicing.unison_sigma_scale` and the whole `[noise]` section, with the engine's `#[serde(default)]`/`skip_serializing_if` behaviour and its field *order* copied exactly — the order is what decides the bytes, and `presets/default.toml` has to round-trip through both crates unchanged. The existing byte-for-byte test cannot see any of it, because a file at the defaults contains none of it; so `a_preset_that_uses_every_refinement_round_trips_through_the_engine` has the engine write a preset with all five present and asserts this module reads it, validates it and writes the same bytes back. `both_crates_refuse_the_same_broken_voicing` grows from 12 mutations to 37, covering every new field on both sides: a NaN or out-of-range width, spread, scale or centroid, a sigma-scale row that no longer averages to 1, a level above 0 dB, anchors out of order, and two `B4` values that fold A0's own partial series — one under the root and one merely back down. Validation is duplicated rather than shared for `DECISIONS.md` 57's reason, and the duplication now includes the engine's *partial layout*: `PartialLayout` recomputes `1 + B k^2 + B4 k^4` and the bank's partial count in `f32`, term for term as `engine::string` computes them, because a series that only just stays ordered lives exactly where an `f64` paraphrase would disagree.

119. **`B4` is fitted as one more term of the same linear problem, behind a guard that is the two-band diagnostic `TUNING_REPORT.md` §1 was written from.** Squaring the layout and dividing by `k^2` turns `f_k = k f0 sqrt(1 + B k^2 + B4 k^4)` into a *parabola* in `x = k^2` — constant term `f0^2`, linear coefficient `f0^2 B`, quadratic `f0^2 B4` — so the estimator that was a weighted least-squares line is a weighted least-squares polynomial, with the same Theil-Sen seed and the same rejection loop around it. Nothing about the two-parameter path changes: `terms = 2` is the old fit, and a note that does not pass the guard gets it. The guard is §1's own statistic — `B` fitted to partials 1–8 and again to 9 and up — with an uncertainty attached to each band by leave-one-out jackknife rather than by a formula, because what makes a band's `B` uncertain is usually one partial rather than the sample size. A quartic is fitted only where the ratio of the two stands at least two of those standard deviations from 1, and is then required to be a *layout*: `B` non-negative (the engine's table is), the series ordered over the partials that were fitted, and the `k^4` term under 60 % of the `k^2` term at the top of them. A fit that is not one is **shrunk** rather than dropped — the coefficient is scaled towards zero and `(f0, B)` refitted against what is left, by bisection on the largest fraction that is still a layout — because in the wound bass the shape §1 measures is larger than one `k^4` term can carry without folding the top of the series, and a partial correction there is a better instrument than none. It is also the rule `PresetBuilder` applies to an interpolated coefficient (item 121), so what the estimator reports is what the file can hold. Where even that comes back at zero, the two-parameter answer is reported with `B4` an exact zero.

The high band is *not* §1's. The report compared partials 1–8 with 14–26; this compares 1–8 with everything above 8 that the fit used, and the difference was measured rather than chosen: what a `k^4` term does to a series grows as `k^4`, so the lever is at the very top of it. On a C2 rendered with a known coefficient the guard stands 2.1 sigma from a flat ratio with the high band running to partial 40, 1.3 with §1's 14–26 window, and 1.1 with 9–26. Only the first fires, so only the first can measure anything — and the consequence, recorded here because it is the kind of thing that otherwise gets discovered twice, is that the ratios this estimator prints are not the ones §1 published for the same recordings: on Salamander's A0, C1 and D#1 it returns 1.01–1.03 at 0.1–0.3 sigma where §1 returned 0.63–0.75. Two windows over two weightings of the same partials; which is the better estimator of the curvature is open, and the one that recovers a coefficient it was given is this one. Measured on the engine's own renders: a C2 rendered at `+3.0e-8` comes back `+3.06e-8` (+2.0 %) and one at `-2.0e-8` comes back `-1.91e-8` (−4.4 %), against the 10 % the gate asks; both cut the fit's residual from 1.7–3.0 cents to 0.65–0.68. The controls — the same notes with the coefficient at zero — come back at a band ratio of 0.98 and a hard `B4 = 0`.

120. **Three separate things bound how much of §1's shape a `B4` can carry, and none of them is the fit.** (a) **The tracker's window.** A `B4` displaces partial 40 by `600 B4 k^4 / (B k^2 ln 2)` cents against the two-parameter seed the second tracking pass uses, and the tracker associates a peak within 60 cents (120 in the survey). At C2 `+3e-8` is 51 cents there and is tracked; `+6e-8` is over a hundred, the top of the series is followed onto the wrong peaks, and the fit comes back with a *worse* residual and no `B4` at all. That is the ceiling on what this pipeline can measure, and it is a property of the seed loop rather than of the estimator. (b) **The engine's bank.** A0 and A1 are built with all eighty partials, so a negative `B4` past about `-2.6e-8` at A1 takes `1 + B k^2 + B4 k^4` under zero at the top of a bank the note actually uses, and both crates refuse it. The wound-bass side of §1's finding is therefore only partly reachable — one `k^4` term buys part of that shape, not all of it. (c) **The note's own noise.** At A1 the same coefficients that C2 resolves at 2.1–2.4 sigma stand at 1.3–1.7, because A1's high band is measured half as well (a jackknife sigma of 0.11–0.15 on the ratio against C2's 0.06–0.08), so the guard reports zero. All three are recorded in the gate's own comments; the gate asserts C2 and deliberately does not assert A1.

121. **An interpolated `B4` is shrunk towards zero rather than allowed to refuse a survey's preset.** `PresetBuilder` fills the `B4` table by interpolating between measured notes exactly like every other table, which means keys nobody measured get a coefficient nobody measured — and a large negative one on a key with eighty partials is a preset the engine will not load, discovered at the end of a run that took minutes. After filling, every key whose series is not ordered has its `B4` halved until it is (terminating at zero, which always is). Shrinking and not refusing, because the two-parameter law is what that key had before and is a valid instrument; shrinking and not clamping to a computed bound, because the bound is not analytic once the bank's own partial count moves with the coefficient. The same rule keeps `contact_width` and `B4` interpolating sanely at the ends of the compass for free: both tables are zero in the base preset, so `fill`'s "move the base curve onto the measurement" rule degenerates to holding the nearest measured value, which is what a correction wants and a curve shape does not.

122. **A contact width is only searched where its own null falls inside the measured spectrum.** For `k w << 1` the taper `cos^2(k pi w / 2)` is `1 - (k pi w / 2)^2` — a smooth droop, which is precisely what the strike fit's envelope polynomial exists to absorb. Measured: a 24-partial spectrum with *no* taper in it and an ordinary felt rolloff gives up 13 % of its residual to a spurious 1.3 % width, and 70 % of it at 16 partials. The only part of the taper an exponential-of-a-polynomial envelope cannot imitate is the null at `k w = 1`, so the width grid starts at `1 / k_max` and a note whose partials do not reach that far is fitted as a point force and says so by returning `None`. A width that is searched must additionally remove 10 % of the residual before it is reported. The result is a fit that recovers 0.02, 0.03 and 0.04 from 60 partials to within 5 %, recovers a rendered 3 % contact at C2 to +14.9 % through the whole engine, and returns `None` on every point-force spectrum tried — including the ones it used to spend a width on.

123. **The contact taper goes on the comb's side of the division, which is why fitting it moves the felt.** `StrikeFit::comb_at` — the factor `LayerSpectrum::from_decays` divides out of every measured amplitude before the felt sees it — now includes the taper. That is where it belongs physically: the taper is how the hammer couples to the string's modes, not part of the force pulse the felt produces, so leaving it in the spectrum makes the felt fit explain it with stiffness and exponent instead. Measured at C2 rendered with a 3 % contact, five velocity layers, the engine's own master chain divided out: the felt fit's residual falls from 4.61 dB to 3.69 dB when the width is fitted. The felt *exponent* is not improved by it — 2.21 with the width against 2.60 without, either side of the truth's 2.42 — which is the honest half of the result and is printed by the gate rather than asserted.

124. **The per-string decay spread is inverted from the composite partial's drift against *time*, and what is inverted is the measurement rather than the model.** Two strings a unison width `d` apart decaying at `sigma (1 -+ s)` are one peak, and a magnitude peak picker lands on their amplitude-weighted centre, so the tracked frequency moves by `(d/2) tanh(ln(a2/a1) / 2)` as the slow one takes over — `TUNING_REPORT.md` §6's signature, and a closed form for `s` given the drift and the width the unison estimator already measures. Two departures from the obvious implementation, each worth about a quarter of the answer. **Against time, not against level.** `residual::track_glide` regresses a partial's frequency against its own level, which is right for a pitch glide and wrong here: a unison's beat period is comparable to the 20 dB window (2.5 s against 2–4 s at C4), so over that window the level is not a clock, and using it as one returns a spread 25 % light on synthetic groups. The window's *end* is still set by level — the last frame above −20 dB, not the first below it, because a beat null takes the envelope down and brings it back. **Predicting the measurement.** What the estimator reports is the interval between the ends of a least-squares line through bin medians, and a straight line through a `tanh` does not pass through its ends; the inversion therefore puts the same line through the same bins of the same `tanh` and inverts *that*. Synthetic groups then come back within 10–15 % at `s` of 0.15 and 0.3.

125. **On the engine's own renders that estimator is good to a factor of two, and the reason is a second pair inside the same partial.** The inversion divides the drift by the unison's width because that is the interval stage 1 can measure. It is not the only interval in there: the engine's two polarizations are offset by 0.27–0.52 Hz — 1.8–3.4 cents at C4, wider than the 2.83-cent unison — and they decay at very different rates by construction, so they hand over at the same time and over a wider interval. A C4 rendered with a 25 % spread comes back at 0.45 (+79 %), and a C4 rendered with *one* damping law still drifts −0.92 cents, because its strings are coupled through the bridge and its polarizations are a pair of their own. The gate therefore asserts two different kinds of thing: that the *signature* is unambiguous (putting the spread in moves the fundamental's drift by +1.11 cents, against a baseline of −0.92), and that the *size* lands within a factor of two once that baseline is named. `SpreadConfig::baseline_cents` is where a caller with a control puts it; the survey leaves it at zero, because a recording of somebody else's piano does not come with one. What the estimator writes is the character of the voicing, bounded — making it a coefficient needs the polarization offsets, which stage 1 does not measure.

126. **The spread is pooled per unison size and written as `[1-s, 1, 1+s]`, magnitude only.** The engine has one row per group size for the whole instrument, so the per-note measurements are pooled — by their own group size where at least three notes of that size were measured, by the instrument's median otherwise, and as ones where nothing was. The row is symmetric about 1 by construction, so it averages to exactly 1 and stays inside the engine's `0.5..=2.0` without a clamp having to do anything. The sign is deliberately dropped: which string of a group survives is a per-note fact — §6's own table runs from −31.9 cents at F#3 to +3.7 at A4 — and a global row cannot carry it, so the row records how far apart the rates are and lets `unison_layout` decide which detuning is the slow one. A note whose drift is under a quarter of a cent contributes a measured **zero** to the pool rather than dropping out of it, which is what stops the median from being taken over only the notes that happened to drift — and a note that *saturated* contributes nothing at all. Saturation means the drift is wider than the model can explain at any spread, which is a statement that the model does not describe that note rather than a measurement of the largest one; counting it as the ceiling would make a median of a clamp. On Salamander that is the whole difference: 13 of its 19 three-string notes and 3 of its 6 two-string notes drift further than their own unison width, and pooling them at the ceiling writes a ±45 % row where the notes the model does fit say ±12 %. The saturated notes are counted separately and printed, because on that library they are the most interesting number in the table — a piano whose measured composite pitches move by more than its unisons are wide is either voiced in a way this model does not contain or is telling us something about the library, which is exactly the open question `TUNING_REPORT.md` §6 left.

127. **The mechanism recordings are indexed by what the SFZ says they are, not by what they are called.** `library.rs` gains `MechanismSample`, and it separates the action from the strings on two markers, both of which are statements about how the sample must be *played*: a release region with `pitch_keytrack=0` is a sample the player is told not to transpose, which is what an unpitched noise is (Salamander's `//HammerNoise` group), and a release region without it is the string still ringing (`harmLC3` and friends, which `TUNING_REPORT.md` §5 measured separately and which are the halo, not the action). A region gated on CC 64 rather than on a key is the pedal, and which way the gate opens — `on_locc64 >= 64` against `on_hicc64 <= 63` — says which direction the tray moved. No filename is matched anywhere, which is the same rule the note index has followed since `library.rs` was written. `Sample` and `MechanismSample` both carry the SFZ's `volume`, because a level is only comparable between groups once it is applied: Salamander attenuates its key-off group by 37 dB and its pedal groups by 19–20, and comparing the raw files would say a damper landing is as loud as the note.

128. **A key-off level is quoted against the nearest key the library actually struck.** Salamander ships 88 key-off recordings and 30 sampled keys, so two thirds of them have no strike of their own to be measured against. `SampleLibrary::nearest_layer` falls back to the nearest key that has one and the measurement reports which key that was; both quantities move smoothly over the couple of semitones involved, and the alternative is measuring two thirds of the compass against nothing. The 88 per-key levels are then reduced to one anchor per octave, each the median of the keys around it rather than that key's own measurement — the measured levels are not smooth (neighbouring octaves differ by 10 dB, and a single clipped file would otherwise become an anchor), and the preset interpolates between anchors anyway.

129. **`velocity_db` is written from the SFZ's `amp_veltrack` under a ceiling, and the ceiling is the only judgement in `estimate::noise`.** SFZ's `amp_veltrack` is a percentage of a `40 log10(v / 127)` law applied to the *note-on* velocity that started the release sample; the engine's `velocity_db` is the range of the same event over *release* velocity. They are different gestures, and a law measured on one is a bound on the other rather than a value for it — Salamander's 82 % over the span of its own sixteen recorded layers comes to nearly 40 dB, which no key release spans and which would make a gentle release inaudible. What is written is that figure capped at 24 dB, and both the number and the reason are in the module. Everything else in the section is a measurement: level, decay to −40 dB and centroid come out of `residual::transient_metrics`, the same function `TUNING_REPORT.md` §5's table was written with, and an event the library never recorded — the damper *lift*, which no library records because a lift under a hammer blow is inaudible — is inherited from the measured key-off by the engine default's own offsets rather than invented.

130. **The survey fits the hammer's contact width and does not write it, for exactly the reason it does not write the strike position.** Both come out of the same comb, and a close microphone's own `sin(k pi x)` comb is not separable from the hammer's (`survey.rs`'s module header, `DECISIONS.md` 93): what the fit returns on library material is right to divide out of an excitation spectrum — which is what the felt fit does with it — and wrong to put in a preset. `NoteSurvey::contact_width` reports it. The fourth-order inharmonicity *is* written, by majority of the note's own layers: each layer decides for itself whether its series curves away from the two-parameter law by more than its own noise, and a note where most of them say it does gets the median of theirs, while a note where most say it does not is measured to have no `B4` and gets a zero. One lucky layer out of sixteen cannot put a coefficient in the file.

131. **The tracker loses a partial in the wound bass, and everything above it is one index too low — which is most of what `TUNING_REPORT.md` §1 measured.** A0's spectrum half a second after a loud strike has peaks at 509.4, 539.6, 601.4, 632.9, 664.7, 696.7, 729.5, 762.2, 795.1, 829.1 and 862.8 Hz; tracks 18 to 24 land on the ones below 700 Hz to within a cent (509.0, 539.6, 601.3, 632.8, 664.6, 696.0), track 25 lands at 750.8 — twenty-one hertz above the peak at 729.5 and twelve below the one at 762.2, on neither — and from track 26 up every track is the partial *above* its own index. The frequencies are real and the numbering is not. It happens where it must: the tracker refuses a peak more than `spacing_fraction` (0.45) of the local spacing from its prediction, so as soon as the model it is predicting from stands more than about half a spacing from the truth, the right peak is outside the window and whatever weak peak is inside it wins. At A0 the two-parameter model is 31 cents flat by partial 24, and half a spacing there is 38. Fitting the mis-numbered top is what gave A0 the coefficient the preset wrote, and with it the 78 cents of misplaced *fundamental* §1 reports; the report's own diagnostic saw the same thing from the other side, since `B` fitted to partials 14–26 (which are numbered correctly) against 1–8 is the 0.63–0.75 ratio, while `B` fitted to everything above 8 (which is not) came back at 1.01–1.03.

132. **The inharmonicity fit trims the measured series at the first skipped partial, and a skip is told from a pulled partial by the step *across* it.** `estimate::inharmonic::trusted_prefix`: a skip leaves one step of two spacings where every other step is one, and stiffness widens the spacing by under 3 % per partial anywhere on this instrument, so half a spacing of margin is unambiguous. The check is not on the wide step alone, because a partial merely *pulled* off its peak — by a neighbour's sidelobe or by a resonance sitting on it — also makes one wide step, and that case belongs to the rejection loop and must not cost the note the rest of its series. What separates them is that a pulled partial comes back at the next index: the step across it spans two spacings, where a skip's spans three. Only consecutive indices are compared, so a partial that was never tracked at all is not evidence of anything. Three tests: a series with a synthetic skip returns `B` to within 2 % where the untrimmed fit is 5 % or more out, a pulled partial leaves the prefix whole, and a missing partial leaves it whole.

133. **With the top of the series gone, the two-band guard fires on the wound bass and reproduces `TUNING_REPORT.md` §1's own ratios.** The guard's high band is everything above partial 8 that the fit used, which is now everything below the skip: A0, C1 and D#1 come back at **0.80/0.68/0.65** against §1's 0.75/0.66/0.63, where before the trim the same statistic said 1.01/1.03/1.01 and no quartic was fitted anywhere in the bass. The open question `DECISIONS.md` 119 left — two windows over the same partials disagreeing, with no way to say which was the better estimator of the curvature — is closed by measurement rather than by preference: the window that disagreed with §1 was reading partials whose index was wrong.

134. **A `B4` fitted over 25 partials is halved until the engine's whole eighty-partial bank stays ordered, and what that costs is recorded rather than hidden.** The fit returns −7.6e−8 at A0, −8.2e−8 at C1 and −7.1e−8 at D#1; `PresetBuilder`'s shrink (`DECISIONS.md` 121) writes −3.8e−8, −2.0e−8 and −1.8e−8, a half and two quarters. The constraint is not the fit's: it is that `f_k` must increase over every partial the engine *builds*, and A0's bank is eighty partials deep while the recording stops being trustworthy at twenty-five. The same `k^4` term that removes 12 cents of error at partial 20 moves partial 80 by several hundred, into a region no measurement reaches — which is the honest cost of putting a local Taylor correction in a global coefficient, and the reason the shrink is a feature rather than a bug. Whether the top of a wound bass string's bank wants a different law at all is a stage-2 question; what is settled here is that the partials the ear resolves individually (A0's 14–26 sit at 390–760 Hz) are now laid out to a few cents instead of tens.

135. **`presets/default.toml` no longer renders the demo byte for byte as the engine that had no action did, and that break is deliberate.** Two of Phase E's findings change the *sound* of the default instrument and not merely its options: the mechanism is on by default because its neutral value is a measurement (`DECISIONS.md` 108), and a damper on its way down soft-limits the string it is landing on (116). Measured, the demo's samples now differ from commit `16307c4`'s render by −12.7 dB RMS, first at 1.90 s, which is the first note-off. Realism is the product and the alternative — shipping the action switched off so that a hash would not move — would be shipping the wrong instrument. What is gated instead is the *sounding* path on its own: `engine/tests/preset.rs`'s `the_sounding_path_is_what_it_was_before_the_mechanism` renders ten strikes across the compass with no release and no pedal move, so no damper is ever between its seats and no event ever fires, and fingerprints the samples against the value that engine produced. It has not moved: `63686423443ec4d3`. A failure there is not a constant to update; it means something reached the strings.

136. **An event quieter than 1e−9 of full scale is not rendered at all, so a preset can silence the mechanism exactly rather than nearly.** `Burst::trigger` already refused a non-positive amplitude; the floor makes `level_db = -200` — which the acceptance suite and `the_mechanism_stays_under_the_music` use to render the instrument without its action — produce *no* burst instead of one at 2e−12, which is the difference between a comparison that can be `assert_eq!` and one that cannot. A velocity-90 strike peaks at `STRIKE_PEAK` = 0.0199 in the same units, so the floor is 166 dB under one strike and nothing audible is refused.

137. **The pan spread is estimated by inverting a line measured on the engine, not by predicting the drift from the model.** What a spread of `s` does to a note's measured stereo drift depends on the key's own pan, on how much of it arrives through the diffuse field (which is not panned per key and compresses every balance towards the middle) and on how far apart the two polarizations' decay rates are — three things the engine knows and the tuner would have to mirror to predict, for a parameter with one number in it. `estimate::directivity` measures §5's statistic on the recordings (median over partials of `|Δ(2 s) − Δ(0.3 s)|`, partials located once on the mono sum so both channels are read at the same frequencies) and maps it through `DRIFT_AT_ZERO_DB + DRIFT_PER_SPREAD_DB * s`, whose two constants were measured over eight keys of the engine's own renders and are checked against it — not against themselves — in `tuner/tests/calibration.rs`. The measurement is at the loudest layer of each sampled key and not at all sixteen, because a soft note's high partials are in the recording's floor by two seconds and the floor has a balance of its own. It is also the one part of the survey that cannot come from the trajectory cache, which holds the mono sum.

138. **Salamander asks for more stereo drift than the engine can produce, and the ceiling is written rather than the estimate.** The median drift over the twenty-eight sampled keys that carry three measurable partials at two seconds is 4.40 dB, which the line puts at a spread of 0.49; `MAX_PAN_SPREAD` is 0.4, where the outer polarization of the outermost key would already be hard left or hard right. The clamp is reported by the survey in the same line as the estimate, because a parameter at its ceiling is a bound and not a measurement. What the engine gets right is the *direction* of the compass: the measured drift climbs from 1.2-1.9 dB across the bottom octave and a half to 4-8 dB from D#2 up, and the engine's at the ceiling climbs the same way (0.24 dB at A0, 1.27 at A2, 3.62 at C4, 8.67 at C5), so the treble now moves further than the recordings and the bass still barely moves — an equal-power pan cannot displace a note that is already panned 0.6 to the left by very much, and pushing the ceiling higher would only make the treble worse.

139. **The fitted contact widths are not a recording-chain artefact, and they are still not written.** The survey now reports the width beside the strike position it comes from. Salamander's are 0.025 of the speaking length at A0, 0.018–0.028 from F#1 to D#3, 0.041 at A3 and 0.050 at C4 — a *fraction* that rises towards the treble, which is what a hammer of roughly fixed physical width does as the speaking length shrinks under it. That also rules out the cheapest explanation: a fixed high-frequency rolloff in the recording chain would put the taper's null `k w = 1` at the same frequency for every note, and these nulls stand at 1.1 kHz (A0), 2.7 kHz (C2) and 5.2 kHz (C4). What it does not rule out is `DECISIONS.md` 93's microphone comb, which is per-note geometry and not a global curve, so `notes.contact_width` stays out of the file and the width goes on being what the felt fit divides out of an excitation spectrum. The evidence that would change this is a second library of the same instrument at a different microphone distance, which stage 1 does not have.

140. **Renumbering the series past a skip was built, measured and thrown away; the fit truncates instead.** If exactly one partial is missing, every index above it is one too low and the cure looks obvious: divide each step by the local spacing, round, and add what is missing to everything above. Measured on Salamander's bass it is worse than useless. At C1 it renumbers 41 of 65 partials and carries the top of the series from 78 to 92, and the two-parameter fit on the result leaves 43 cents where the truncated fit leaves 3; at C2 and A2, where the truncated fit is already inside 6 cents, the loudest layer picks up renumberings that move it to 15–16, and at A1 to 24. The reason is that the rounding compounds: the local spacing is itself measured from the series being corrected, the high partials are irregular enough that one wrong round shifts everything above it, and there is no second opinion anywhere in the data. Truncation throws away information and cannot be wrong about the information it keeps, which is the trade a fit wants. The measurement stays in this log so the idea does not get rebuilt.

141. **What the milestone bought and what it cost, in one place.** Against the partials whose index is certain, the layout `presets/salamander-c5.toml` writes moved from 12.32 to 8.79 cents RMS at A0, 12.27 to 6.33 at C1 and 10.54 to 4.57 at D#1 (the twelfth of sixteen velocity layers; worst partial 37.1 to 38.5, 31.6 to 16.6 and 24.0 to 11.3); §1's published figures for the same keys were 15.7, 13.1 and 15.4 over the *whole* tracked series, which is the number that cannot be compared any more because two thirds of it is mis-numbered. Above the skip — real partials 26 and up, at 700–1800 Hz and 40–60 dB down — the same layout is *worse*: taking the numbering to be one out, A0 goes from 51.8 cents RMS to 128.1 and D#1 from 42.7 to 99.5, while C1 barely moves (54.9 to 57.9). That is the `k^4` term's extrapolation, and it is why 134 records the shrink as a feature. Two things keep it from being a straight regression: the region was already 40–60 cents wrong before, and the assumption that the offset up there is exactly one partial is the same one 140 could not make reliable. A wound bass string's partials above the twenty-fifth are, on this evidence, not described by any two-coefficient law and not measurable by this tracker; that is a stage-2 finding with a stage-2 fix (track the bass against a fitted series rather than a seeded one, and re-track), not a preset field.

142. **Independent audio verification of Milestone A (`tuner/examples/verify_milestone_a.rs`), and the four things it found beside the confirmations.** Every claim was re-measured from rendered audio and the cached Salamander trajectories rather than taken from the gate. Confirmed: C1 and D#1 trusted-prefix placement (6.29 / 4.66 cents median over the 16 layers, against 141's 6.33 / 4.57 for one layer); stereo drift 0.24–8.67 dB at spread 0.4 falling to 0.01–0.72 at spread 0 (138's numbers, independently); pedal noise within ~1 dB of §5's table (down −35.6 vs −35.8, up −43.6 vs −42.4), centroids and decays on the table (pedal-down decay is **6.09 s by envelope-slope fit** — `transient_metrics` returns 2.56 s on the same render because a narrowband noise fade crosses −40 dB early, so that function should not be used for noise decays); the >2 kHz band limit at −40 to −44 dB for every event; bit-identical repeated renders; no NaN, no samples at 1.0, DC below −177 dBFS in 45 s of dense playing. The findings: (a) **A0's median trusted residual is 16.8 cents, not 141's 8.79** — 141's figure is layer 11 (vel 92) and reproduces exactly, but the median over all sixteen layers is dominated by the *fundamental's own track*, which lands −18 to −531 cents off at a level of −27 to −44 dB re the third partial; excluding k=1, A0's median is ≈5.3 cents. The A0 fundamental row of §1's table measures the tracker in most layers, not the string. (b) **Key-off noise renders 0.4–4.9 dB above what the preset asks** (mean +3.3 dB over five keys, four bursts averaged per key, stereo-magnitude peaks so pan cancels): ~1.5 dB of it is `noise::STRIKE_PEAK` being a constant calibrated on the default preset while salamander-c5's vel-90 strikes peak 1.4–1.9 dB lower, and the default preset shows the same-shaped excess (mean +2.4 dB) inside its own acceptance tolerance. Against §5's *per-key* table the render stands −1.3 to +7.8 dB, the +7.8 at C4, because the preset's anchors are octave medians (128) sitting up to 5 dB from individual keys. (c) **The silently-prepared string is audible only while its exciter rings**: held-minus-control on a C3-under-G4 render is ≈−100 dBFS RMS while G4 sounds (≈80 dB under the strike), and collapses to −160 then −282 dBFS within a second of G4's damping — the picked-up energy sits below the idle/cull thresholds, so pedal-up preparation never audibly *rings on*, which is the musical point of the gesture. The acceptance test asserts bank energy, which this does not contradict. (d) **The one systematic derivative outlier is the key-off burst's own onset**: `Burst::trigger` restarts the envelope at full amplitude, so the noise reaches peak within a sample — 11–13× the local derivative RMS at an absolute −57 to −60 dBFS, both channels, at every exposed release (loud treble hammer attacks reach 12–15× as the honest baseline). Under the 12× bar in the phrase test and inaudible under music; a 1–2 ms attack ramp would remove it if exposed-silence releases ever tick. Also measured: the fitted `unison_sigma_scale` row moves C4's fundamental drift (1 s→6 s) from +3.82 to +2.28 cents and a ±30 % row to −0.85 — monotone in the row's width — while F#3 barely moves at the fitted ±12 %; both are far short of the −31.9 cents the recordings show at F#3, which is 126's saturation finding seen from the audio side.

143. **The felt limits a string only while the damper is *arriving*, and the peak follower runs whenever the felt is not limiting.** Item 116 designed the soft limiter for a damper coming down — a release, a half pedal — but `felt_in_contact` asked only whether the damper was somewhere between its two end positions, and it is also between them for the first ~10 ms of every re-strike: the engine starts the damper's lift and the hammer's blow at the same instant, where the real action lifts the damper early in the key's travel and has it clear before the hammer arrives. The threshold it limited against was `felt_reference`, which the follower stopped updating the moment `damper_current > 0` and which therefore still held the level the *previous* note had when its damper landed. Measured on a C4 re-struck at velocity 120 three seconds after a released note, mechanism silenced, in 3 ms windows against the identical strike from silence: **−68.0, −52.2, −6.2, −1.6 dB** when the previous note was pianissimo and **−45.0, −27.1, −6.0, −1.6 dB** when it was fortissimo — the whole attack transient choked, by an amount that depended on how loud the note before it had been (25 dB of history at 3–6 ms). Every staccato repetition of a key was affected, including the first section of `renders/salamander-ab/mechanism_demo.wav`, and no test could see it: the fingerprint gate's probe contains no releases at all. `felt_is_limiting` now also requires `damper_target >= damper_current` — the felt limits while it arrives or rests in contact, never while it leaves — and the same predicate gates `track_felt_reference`, so the follower picks up the *new* note's free swing during the lift instead of preserving a dead one's, and the reference decays while a voice sits damped and idle instead of standing at the last level it ever reached. The re-strike now renders within **0.0–0.3 dB** of the strike from silence in every window, with no dependence on the previous note left at the 0.1 dB the measurement resolves; the test allows 1.0 dB against the clean strike and 0.5 dB between a pianissimo and a fortissimo predecessor. Nothing about the wanted case moves: a half pedal has `damper_target >= damper_current` throughout its arrival and at rest, and `acceptance::a_half_pedalled_note_is_not_merely_a_faster_decay` is unchanged. The new `acceptance::a_restrike_attacks_like_a_strike_from_silence` fails by 68 dB on the old predicate. Audio-path cost: the same comparison plus a block peak for damped voices that used to skip it — worst case 39.1 % of one core against 39.5 % before.

144. **A burst that has stopped does not lend its filter state to the next event, and that was worth 1.4 dB.** `Burst::trigger` keeps the band-pass states when it retriggers, which is right while the previous event is still ringing — item 109's trill that must not click. It was also being done when the previous event was long dead, and the states a dead burst leaves behind are not small: the filters run on *un-enveloped* white noise and only their sum is scaled by the envelope, so a burst that has faded to nothing still leaves states of order one. Released into the next event they are a foreign transient arriving exactly where its peak is. Measured on the gesture that produces it — a silently pressed key, whose damper-lift burst is 0.22 s dead by the time the key is released: the release peaked **1.39 dB** above the mean of the identical release on a key that had never been touched, and rendered differently from the burst `EventShape::new` normalised. With the states cleared when the burst is not active, the two are bit-identical (`noise::tests::a_burst_that_follows_a_finished_one_starts_from_silence`, which also asserts that a burst retriggered *while it rings* still keeps its state). This is why 142(b)'s measurement of the key-off level read hot by more than the level error alone: its protocol pressed each key silently 0.4 s before releasing it. It also takes most of 142(d) with it — the click scan's largest derivative ratio at an exposed release is now 10.6 (left) and 11.4 (right) against the 11–13 it reported, under the 12 the phrase test bars, because the transient it was measuring was partly the previous burst's state arriving in one sample.

145. **A mechanism level is a ratio to a strike at the ear, so both halves of it are now measured there — per preset, per key and per event shape.** `TUNING_REPORT.md` §5 quotes every event as a peak relative to a velocity-90 strike of the same key, measured on both sides at the microphone, and the engine's thump reaches the ear through the board exactly as the strike does (item 110) — so the ratio the preset asks for is a ratio of two *outputs*. The engine anchored it at the board's *input* instead, against `STRIKE_PEAK = 0.0199`, a constant measured once on the default preset; what that leaves in the answer is the board's own response — it passes a 190 Hz burst 1.3 dB more generously than a C4 strike's peak and 3.4 dB more than an A1's — plus whatever the loaded preset's gain staging does, and salamander-c5's strikes peak 1.4–1.9 dB below the default's. Measured over nine keys, 16 events each, against what each preset asks for: **default +2.31 dB mean (−0.4 to +5.6), salamander-c5 +3.88 (+0.4 to +5.9)** — and the 1.6 dB between the two presets was the constant being right about only one of them. The new `engine/src/calibrate.rs` measures, when the engine is built, the output peak of a velocity-90 strike at eight keys across the compass (50 ms of one voice and one board; the peak of a strike is within 0.00 dB of its 3-second value by then, and interpolating between octaves is worth ~0.2 dB) and the mean output peak of eight realizations of each event shape, and hands `EventModel::new` `strike_peak(key) / board_gain(shape)` in place of the constant. Same nine keys: **default −0.33 dB mean (−1.2 to +0.5), salamander −0.36 (−1.1 to +0.7)** — the two presets now agree to 0.03 dB, which is the property that was actually missing. Pedal-down went from +1.8/+3.8 to −0.3/−0.1, pedal-up from +0.1/+1.6 to −1.1/−1.1; the pedal events are quoted against C4's strike, which is the key `estimate::noise` quotes them against. What is left is one sampling error: the burst's own normalisation cancels out of the product exactly, so the accuracy of the design level is the accuracy of estimating a mean output peak from eight realizations of a quantity that scatters by 5–7 dB, which is about half a decibel — the shipped instrument renders 0.8 dB under the ask across the compass, against the 2–3 dB by which each individual event is deliberately allowed to scatter (item 114) — and `acceptance::a_note_off_thumps_at_the_level_the_recordings_measured` tightens from 6 dB per key and 2.5 dB mean to **2.0 and 1.2**, now asserted against the level the *preset* asks for rather than against the report's table, which makes it a claim about the engine that holds for any piano loaded into it. Item 136's silence floor is unaffected — the measured reference is still a few hundredths of full scale, so `SILENT_AMPLITUDE` stands some 160 dB under one strike — and the constant it was named against is gone. Cost: `Engine::new` goes from 4.4 ms to 15.9 ms, all of it setup and three quarters of it the four board-gain measurements; nothing is measured after construction returns.

146. **The silently prepared string is inaudible because of the coupling level, not because of the culling — measured by moving the level.** 142(c) found C3's held-minus-control contribution collapsing within a second of the note that excited it being damped, and asked whether the idle and cull thresholds were cutting the gesture off. They are not, and the way to tell is to raise the one number that is a stage-2 parameter. At the shipped `resonance_coupling` (0.012) C3 answers a fortissimo G4 at **−95 dBFS** RMS and falls to −114 dBFS within half a second of G4's damper landing, at −130 half a second later, gone after that: it never stands more than five decibels above the engine's own audibility floor (`IDLE_ENERGY`, −100 dBFS at the master output; `CULL_AMPLITUDE`, −90 dB per mode), so there is nothing left for it to ring on with. At the maximum coupling a preset may ask for (0.05) the same gesture picks up **−83 dBFS** and does ring on — −103 and −104 dBFS in the two half-seconds *after* G4 is damped, decaying at its own rate — until it too crosses the floor and is dropped. The pedal-down halo, which sits at −49 to −64 dBFS, runs the full six seconds of the render with no truncation at all. So the thresholds truncate only what is already ten decibels under the floor they are built to be, and the audio-level gap is `TUNING_REPORT.md`'s backlog item 5: the picked-up level sits about 75 dB under a strike of the same key, where §5's `harmLC3` recording puts the instrument's own aftersound at −31 dB, and that level cannot be fitted from isolated notes. Recorded as a refutation of the diagnosis rather than a fix, with `acceptance::a_prepared_string_rings_on_after_the_note_that_excited_it` pinning the part that *is* the engine's: raise the coupling to where the pickup is audible and the persistence comes with it.
147. **Post-workflow review pass (Claude, 2026-08-14) resolved two open review findings.** (a) ~~A NoteOn at velocity 1–3 was reinterpreted as a silent press (item 111's mechanism)~~ — overturned: any nonzero velocity now throws the hammer, because recorded performances (MAESTRO) carry genuine pianissimo notes at velocities 1–3 and a threshold silences them — a silent, data-dependent loss that stage-2 replay fitting cannot survive. The silent press keeps its explicit spellings (`Event::KeyDown`, REPL `hold`, or a velocity-0 note-on); `ESCAPEMENT_VELOCITY` survives only as the nominal drive of a silent press's damper-lift noise. Pinned by an acceptance assert that a velocity-1 note-on strikes. (b) The B4 radicand validation in both crates now runs over the full reachable partial range (to `MAX_PARTIALS`, stopping at the Nyquist cap) instead of `partial_count()` — the count's own `take_while` stops at the first non-finite frequency, so a radicand that jumped straight negative truncated the bank silently while validating cleanly (`b4[C8] = -0.3` was the demonstrated case; now refused, and `tuner::ordered_series` agrees). Accepted as-is from verification: the sigma-spread T60 tail lengthening (~14 % at fitted spread — revisit when stage 2 fits decay and spread jointly), the treble pan-spread overshoot (a per-register spread is future work), and the single-sample burst attack (under the click bar since the calibration fix).
148. **The bridge admittance is one filter on the bus, not one per string, and what that buys is paid for in the own-contribution subtraction.** `PHYSICS.md` §4's build note says "one shared filter on one mono signal, ~24–40 resonators (0.06 %)" and also that the drive becomes `coupling·B(bus − own)`. Those two cannot both be had. `B` is linear, so `B(bus − own_i) = B(bus) − B(own_i)`, and `B(own_i)` is a different signal for every one of the 88 voices: forming it exactly needs 88 filter *states*, which measured at 0.20 % of a core each (47 sections, 48 kHz) is 18 % for the instrument, against a worst case already at 38.7 % of `SPEC.md`'s 50 % budget. So the filter runs once per block on the mono bus — `ResonanceBus::begin_block` publishes `B(accum)` rather than `accum` — and the drive path became `coupling·(B(bus) − own_gain[key]·own_previous)`, where `own_gain` is `|B|` averaged over that key's own partials, weighted `1/k²` (the amplitude law a struck string's bridge force roughly follows), computed once in `ResonanceBus::from_preset`. The subtraction is therefore exact *in the mean over the frequencies the string actually puts on the bus*, and what survives is the fluctuation of `B` across one note's series. That choice is not cosmetic: a backbone that falls 18 dB across the treble, which is the shape the physics asks for, would otherwise leave every treble string subtracting a full-scale copy of its own output against a bus that carries a tenth of it — a systematic `coupling·(B−1)·own` of extra damping over a third of the compass, produced by nothing but the filter's tilt. `resonance::tests::the_own_gain_follows_the_bridge_across_the_compass` pins that the gain tracks the backbone (+4.9 dB at E1 under a +6 dB backbone, −16.5 dB at A7 under a −18 dB one). With no `[voicing.bridge]` section the filter is `BridgeFilter::unity`, `process` is a `copy_from_slice`, every `own_gain` is exactly 1.0, and the drive is the old expression bit for bit. The only signature that changed is the drive path's, which gained the voice index (`voice.rs`, one line); `string.rs` and `soundboard.rs` are untouched. **The tuner's copy of the schema has not been written yet** — `presets/default.toml` and `presets/salamander-c5.toml` contain no `[voicing.bridge]`, so `tuner/src/preset.rs`'s `deny_unknown_fields` round trip still passes, but a preset that voices the bridge cannot be read by the tuner until it mirrors `BridgeVoicing`, `BridgeAnchor` and `BridgePeak` with the same bounds (items 102 and 118's rule).

149. **The loop bound is computed from the filter that was built, not from the numbers in the file, and it is a quarter of unity.** Item 23's stability argument was written for a flat bus: a string answers a steady drive at one of its own partials with at most about one signal unit per unit drive, so the tightest loop `string → bus → string` has gain `≈ coupling` and bounding `coupling` bounded the loop. `B` is allowed gain well over one, so the quantity that plays that role is now the *effective* coupling `coupling·max|B(f)|`, and `Preset::validate` refuses any preset where it exceeds `MAX_BRIDGE_LOOP_GAIN = 0.25` — a quarter of the gain at which the loop sustains itself, i.e. 12 dB of margin against the very worst string in the instrument (`A ≈ 1`, the slowest bass partial) and four times that again against the realistic case where no two partials coincide exactly. `MAX_COUPLING` still bounds `coupling` on its own, so a unity `B` is exactly as constrained as it was, and `DRIVE_CEILING` is untouched and remains the guarantee that does not depend on any of this. Three details are the difference between a contract and a slogan. (a) `max|B|` is measured on the **realised** cascade — the fitted shelves and the peaking sections that were actually constructed — because the backbone is a fit and forty cascaded peaks multiply: eight +20 dB resonances stacked at one frequency realise **+160 dB**, which the file's own numbers do not say anywhere (`resonance::tests::stacked_peaks_are_refused_by_the_loop_gain_check`). (b) It is measured at 512 log-spaced points from 20 Hz to 20 kHz *and at every peak's own centre frequency*: the grid steps by 1.36 % and the schema's sharpest resonance (`Q = 50`) is 2 % wide, so the grid alone would under-read a hostile preset by several decibels (`the_measured_maximum_sees_the_sharpest_resonance_the_schema_allows`, five frequencies from 20 Hz to 15 kHz). (c) The gate is a render, not an argument: `acceptance::the_most_extreme_bridge_a_preset_may_ask_for_stays_bounded_for_a_minute` builds the most extreme preset the bound admits — forty resonances spread geometrically from 23 Hz to 15 kHz so one lands on a partial of nearly every key, half of them at the `Q` ceiling, on a backbone that swings 34 dB — measures its realised maximum (**+24.1 dB**), sets `resonance_coupling` to the largest value that still validates (0.0156, putting the loop exactly on 0.250), and plays 2400 notes over 60 s with the sustain pedal down and never released. Finite, under 0 dBFS, and quieter after the playing stops than during it — the last assertion because "bounded" alone is also true of a loop that rides the limiter for a minute. Release-only; 24 s to run.

150. **The backbone is fitted to its anchors rather than realised from them, and the first shelf that was tried could not do it.** `[voicing.bridge].backbone` is a curve — gains at frequencies, interpolated in log `f` — and a curve is not a filter. It is built as a broadband gain plus one high shelf per anchor interval, cornered at the interval's geometric mean and carrying the interval's dB step, then refined: 32 passes that measure where the cascade actually passes through each anchor and move the steps by the difference, keeping the best pass rather than the last so a target the fit cannot follow ends at its closest approach. The first attempt used the textbook first-order shelf `(A s + w)/(s + w)`, and it failed for a reason worth recording: that shelf's transition is neither symmetric in log frequency nor as narrow as its step — a decade *below* its corner a 20 dB shelf is already 3 dB up — so a cascade of them cannot be pushed through anchors an octave apart, and the fit on `PHYSICS.md` §4's own target shape came out **3.2 dB** wrong at 4 kHz with the refinement running. The RBJ second-order high shelf at `S = 1` is symmetric about its corner, half the step exactly at it, and roughly as wide as the step it carries; the same target now fits to **0.6 dB** worst case (`the_backbone_passes_through_its_anchors`). What is left is the honest limit: the backbone smooths any feature narrower than about an octave, which is the right failure for a mean-mobility curve — the board's discrete modes are `peaks`, by construction and by Ege & Boutillon's split at 1.1 kHz, and belong in the other list. The sections are `f64`: a `Q`-50 resonance at 20 Hz is a pole 2.6e−5 of the way round the unit circle, where an `f32` coefficient has four significant digits of pole position left, and at one instance on one mono signal the double precision costs nothing measurable.

151. **The decay-rate coupling `PHYSICS.md` §4 expects to fall out free does not fall out, and the measurement says where it has to come from instead.** §4 predicts two consequences of putting `B` on the bus: sympathetic excitation becomes frequency-selective, and the coupling becomes two-way — "the decay-rate coupling". The first is exactly true and large. Two co-tuned one-partial strings, one struck, a ±12 dB resonance under the note: the energy the struck string delivers to its neighbour moves by **+11.8 / −11.6 dB** against the flat bus, i.e. by the resonance's own gain to within two tenths of a decibel, and it does so identically at A0, C4 and C6 and at every coupling from 0.012 to 0.05. That is the halo becoming selective, and it is `resonance::tests::a_partial_on_a_bridge_peak_sheds_energy_into_the_bus_faster_than_one_in_a_trough` (24 dB between peak and trough, two keys 5¼ octaves apart). The second is not true, and cannot be, for a structural reason. With `own` subtracted, nothing in the loop is proportional to the string's own motion, so the bus can only ever *add* drive to a string and never remove energy from it; what remains is the second-order path — the neighbour's answer coming back — whose exchange time is `1/L` note-lengths, about a hundred at these couplings. Measured: even at the loop-gain ceiling and with the resonance sitting on the fundamental, the struck string's own level after five seconds moves by **less than 0.4 dB**, against the 12 dB its halo moves. Nor can the residual self-term supply it: a string's response at its own partial is 90° out of phase with its drive, so self-feedback shifts the partial's *frequency* rather than its damping, and what decides the sign of the small damping component is the bus's one-block delay — which is why the same experiment ends 1.2 dB **down** at A0 over 60 s and 3.2 dB **up** at C4. That is an artefact of a 2.7 ms delay, not a bridge. So the decay-rate half of §4 needs `Re Y` in the string's own per-partial damping (`string.rs`, `partial_sigma`), not in the bus, and it is deliberately out of this change's scope. `resonance::tests::the_bridge_does_not_move_the_struck_strings_own_decay` pins the refutation the way item 100's do — asserting the effect is *absent* — so that the day it is implemented in the string, the test fails and says so.
